# G.6 Proof of Lemma G.1

In order to prove Lemma G.1, we first show that we can represent the Cost functional as a quadratic form in Gaussian variables.

Lemma G.12. Let  $(\mathbf{x}_1, \mathbf{x}_2, \sigma_u)$  denote the linear dynamical system described by the evolution of  $\mathcal{D}(K, x_1)$ . Then for any  $t \geq 1$ , there exists a standard Gaussian? vector  $\overline{\mathbf{g}} \in \mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{O}(td)}$  such that for any cost matrices  $R_1, R_2 \succeq 0$ , we have

$$
\mathsf {C o s t} (R _ {1}, R _ {2}; x _ {1}, \sigma_ {u}, t) = \overline {{\mathbf {g}}} ^ {\top} \Lambda_ {\overline {{\mathbf {g}}}} \overline {{\mathbf {g}}} + x _ {1} ^ {\top} \Lambda_ {\mathbf {x} _ {1}} x _ {1} + 2 \overline {{\mathbf {g}}} ^ {\top} \Lambda_ {\mathrm {c r o s s}} x _ {1},
$$

where, letting  $R_{K} = R_{1} + K^{\top}R_{2}K$ ,  $A_{K} = A_{\star} + B_{\star}K$ ,  $P_{K} = \mathrm{dlyap}(A_{K},R_{K})$ $J_{K}\coloneqq \operatorname {tr}(P_{K})$ , and  $d_{\mathrm{eff}}\coloneqq \min \{d_{\mathbf{u}},\dim (R_1) + \dim (R_2)\}$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathrm {t r} (\Lambda_ {\overline {{\mathbf {g}}}}) \leq t J _ {k} + 2 \sigma_ {u} ^ {2} t d _ {\mathrm {e f f}} \left(\| R _ {2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} + \| B _ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2} \| P _ {K} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}\right), \\ \| \Lambda_ {\overline {{\mathbf {g}}}} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \lesssim \left(1 + \sigma_ {u} ^ {2} \| B _ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2}\right) \| R _ {K} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \| A _ {K} \| _ {\mathcal {H} _ {\infty}} ^ {2} + \sigma_ {u} ^ {2} \| R _ {2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2}, \\ \Lambda_ {\mathbf {x} _ {1}} \preceq P _ {K}, \\ \| \Lambda_ {\text {c r o s s}} x _ {1} \| _ {2} \leq \sqrt {\| \Lambda_ {\overline {{\mathbf {g}}}} \| _ {\text {o p}} \cdot x _ {1} ^ {\top} P _ {K} x}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Let us continue to prove Lemma G.1. The expectation result follows since  $\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{Cost}(R_1,R_2;x_1,\sigma_u,t)] = \mathrm{tr}(\Lambda_{\overline{\mathbf{g}}}) + x_1^\top \Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_1}x_1$  for a Gaussian quadratic form.

For the high probability result, observe that by Gaussian concentration and Lemma G.12, we have with probability  $1 - \delta$

$$
2 \overline {{\mathbf {g}}} ^ {\top} \Lambda_ {\mathrm {c r o s s}} x _ {1}, \lesssim \sqrt {\log (1 / \delta) \| \Lambda_ {\mathrm {c r o s s}} x _ {1} \| _ {2}} \lesssim \sqrt {\log (1 / \delta) \| \Lambda_ {\overline {{\mathbf {g}}}} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \cdot x _ {1} ^ {\top} P _ {K} x _ {1}}.
$$

Hence, by AM-GM,  $2\overline{\mathbf{g}}^{\top}\Lambda_{\mathrm{cross}}x_{1} \leq \mathcal{O}\left(\log (1 / \delta)\| \Lambda_{\overline{\mathbf{g}}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\right) + x_{1}^{\top}P_{K}x_{1}$ . On the other hand, by Hanson-Wright

$$
\begin{array}{l} \overline {{\mathbf {g}}} ^ {\top} \Lambda_ {\overline {{\mathbf {g}}}} \overline {{\mathbf {g}}} \leq \operatorname {t r} \left(\operatorname {t r} \left(\Lambda_ {\overline {{\mathbf {g}}}}\right)\right) + \mathcal {O} \left(\| \Lambda_ {\overline {{\mathbf {g}}}} \| _ {\mathrm {F}} \sqrt {\log (1 / \delta)} + \| \Lambda_ {\overline {{\mathbf {g}}}} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \log (1 / \delta)\right) \tag {G.11} \\ \leq \operatorname {t r} (\Lambda_ {\overline {{\mathbf {g}}}}) + \mathcal {O} \left(\sqrt {t d \log (1 / \delta)} + \| \Lambda_ {\overline {{\mathbf {g}}}} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \log (1 / \delta)\right), \\ \end{array}
$$

where we use the dimension of  $\Lambda_{\overline{\mathbf{g}}}$  in the last line. Combining with the previous result, and adding in  $x_1^\top \Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_1} x_1 \leq x_1^\top P_K x_1$ , we have that with probability  $1 - \delta$ ,

$$
\mathsf {C o s t} (R _ {1}, R _ {2}; x _ {1}, \sigma_ {u}, t) \leq \operatorname {t r} (\Lambda_ {\overline {{\mathbf {g}}}}) + \mathcal {O} \left((\sqrt {t d \log (1 / \delta)} + \log (1 / \delta)) \| \Lambda_ {\overline {{\mathbf {g}}}} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} + x _ {1} ^ {\top} P _ {K} x _ {1}\right).
$$

The first high-probability statement follows by substituting in  $\mathrm{tr}(\Lambda_{\overline{\mathbf{g}}})$  and  $\| \Lambda_{\overline{\mathbf{g}}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}$ . Then second statement follows from returning to Eq. G.11 and using  $\| X\|_{\mathrm{op}}, \| X\|_{\mathrm{F}} \leq \mathrm{tr}(X)$  for  $X \succeq 0$ .

We shall now prove Lemma G.12, but first, we establish some useful preliminaries.

# G.6.1 Linear Algebra Preliminaries

Definition G.5 (Toeplitz Operator). For  $\ell \in \mathbb{N}$ , and  $j, \ell \geq i$ , define the matrices

$$
\mathsf {T o e p} _ {i, j, \ell} (A) := \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c} A ^ {i} \mathbb {I} _ {i \geq 0} & A ^ {i + 1} \mathbb {I} _ {i \geq - 1} & \ldots & A ^ {i + \ell} \mathbb {I} _ {i \geq - \ell} \\ A ^ {i - 1} \mathbb {I} _ {i \geq 1} & A ^ {i} \mathbb {I} _ {i \geq 0} & \ldots & A ^ {i + \ell - 1} \mathbb {I} _ {i \geq 1 - \ell} \\ \ldots & \ldots & \ldots & \ldots \\ A ^ {i - j} \mathbb {I} _ {i \geq j} & \ldots & \ldots & A ^ {i + \ell - j} \mathbb {I} _ {i + \ell - j \geq 0} \end{array} \right], \quad \mathsf {T o e p C o l} _ {i, j} (A) := \left[ \begin{array}{c} A ^ {j - 1} \\ A ^ {j - 2} \\ \ldots \\ \mathbb {I} _ {i \geq 1} A ^ {i - 1} \end{array} \right].
$$

We shall use the following lemma.

Lemma G.13. For any  $i \leq j, \ell$ , we have  $\| \mathrm{ToepCol}_{i,j} \|_{\mathrm{op}} \leq \| \mathrm{Toep}_{i,j,\ell}(A) \|_{\mathrm{op}} \leq \| A \|_{\mathcal{H}_{\infty}}$ , and, for  $Y \in \mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{x}}^2}$ , and  $\mathrm{diag}_{j-i}(Y)$  denoting a  $j-i$ -block block matrix with blocks  $Y$  on the diagonal, we have the bound

$$
\operatorname {t r} \left(\operatorname {T o e p C o l} _ {i, j} (A) ^ {\top} \operatorname {d i a g} _ {j - i} (Y) \operatorname {T o e p C o l} _ {i, j} (A)\right) \preceq (j - i) \cdot \operatorname {t r} (\operatorname {d l y a p} (Y, A))
$$

Proof. The first bound is a consequence of the fact that  $\mathsf{Toep}_{i,j,\ell}(A)$  is a submatrix of the infinite-dimensional linear operator mapping inputs sequences in  $\ell_2(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{x}}})$  to outputs  $\ell_2(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{x}}})$ ; thus, the operator norm of  $\mathsf{Toep}_{i,j,\ell}(A)$  is bounded by the operator norm of this infinite dimensional linear operator, which is equal to  $\| A\|_{\mathcal{H}_\infty}$  (see e.g. Tilli [1998, Corollary 4.2]). The second bound follows from direct computation, as

$$
\operatorname {t r} (\mathsf {T o e p C o l} _ {i, j} (A) ^ {\top} \operatorname {d i a g} _ {j - i} (Y) \mathsf {T o e p C o l} _ {i, j} (A)) \leq \operatorname {t r} (\sum_ {s = 0} ^ {\infty} A ^ {\top} Y A) = \operatorname {t r} (\mathsf {d l y a p} (A, Y)).
$$

# G.6.2 Proof of Lemma G.12

Lemma G.14 (Form of the Covariates). Introduce the vector  $\mathbf{x}_{[t]} = (\mathbf{x}_t,\dots ,\mathbf{x}_1)$  and  $\mathbf{u}_{[t]}\coloneqq$ $(\mathbf{u}_t,\ldots ,\mathbf{u}_1)$ , set  $\overline{\mathbf{w}}_{[t - 1]} = (\mathbf{w}_{t - 1},\ldots ,\mathbf{w}_1)$  and  $\mathbf{g}_{[t]} = (\mathbf{g}_t,\ldots ,\mathbf{g}_1)$ . Then, we can write

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {x} _ {[ t ]} \\ \mathbf {u} _ {[ t ]} \end{array} \right] = M _ {K, t} \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {w} _ {[ t - 1 ]} \\ \mathbf {g} _ {[ t ]} \end{array} \right] + \underbrace {\left[ \begin{array}{c} I _ {t} \\ \operatorname {d i a g} _ {t} (K) \end{array} \right] \operatorname {T o e p C o l} _ {1 , t} (0)} _ {:= M _ {0, t}} \mathbf {x} _ {1},
$$

where we have defined the matrix

$$
M _ {K, t} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \mathsf {T o e p} _ {0, t, t - 1} (A _ {K}) & \sigma_ {u} \mathsf {T o e p} _ {- 1, t, t} (A _ {K}) \mathrm {d i a g} _ {t} (B _ {\star}) \\ K \mathsf {T o e p} _ {0, t, t - 1} (A _ {K}) & \sigma_ {u} \mathrm {d i a g} _ {t} (I) + \sigma_ {u} K \mathsf {T o e p} _ {- 1, t, t} (A _ {K}) \mathrm {d i a g} _ {t} (B _ {\star}) \end{array} \right].
$$

Further, let

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{c} A \\ \hline B \end{array} \right] _ {\text {d i a g}} := \left[ \begin{array}{c c} A & 0 \\ 0 & B \end{array} \right].
$$

In light of the above lemma, we have for  $\overline{\mathbf{g}} := \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf{w}_{[t-1]} \\ \mathbf{g}_{[t]} \end{array} \right]$ , we have that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {s = 1} ^ {t} \mathbf {x} _ {s} ^ {\top} R _ {1} \mathbf {x} _ {s} + \mathbf {u} _ {s} ^ {\top} R _ {2} \mathbf {u} _ {s} \\ = \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {x} _ {[ t ]} \\ \mathbf {u} _ {[ t ]} \end{array} \right] ^ {\top} \left[ \begin{array}{c} \operatorname {d i a g} (R _ {1}) \\ \hline \operatorname {d i a g} (R _ {2}) \end{array} \right] _ {\text {d i a g}} \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {x} _ {[ t ]} \\ \mathbf {u} _ {[ t ]} \end{array} \right] \\ = \left(M _ {K, t} \overline {{\mathbf {g}}} _ {t} + M _ {0, t} \mathbf {x} _ {1}\right) ^ {\top} \left[ \frac {\operatorname {d i a g} (R _ {1})}{\operatorname {d i a g} (R _ {2})} \right] _ {\mathrm {d i a g}} \left(M _ {K, t} \overline {{\mathbf {g}}} _ {t} + M _ {0, t} \mathbf {x} _ {1}\right) \\ = \overline {{\mathbf {g}}} _ {t} ^ {\top} \underbrace {M _ {K , t} ^ {\top} \left[ \frac {\mathrm {d i a g} (R _ {1})}{\mathrm {d i a g} (R _ {2})} \right] _ {\mathrm {d i a g}} M _ {K , t}} _ {:= \Lambda_ {\overline {{\mathbf {g}}}}} + 2 \mathbf {x} _ {1} ^ {\top} \underbrace {M _ {0 , t} ^ {\top} \left[ \frac {\mathrm {d i a g} (R _ {1})}{\mathrm {d i a g} (R _ {2})} \right] _ {\mathrm {d i a g}} M _ {K , t}} _ {:= \Lambda_ {\mathrm {c r o s s}}} \overline {{\mathbf {g}}} _ {t} \\ + \mathbf {x} _ {1} ^ {\top} \underbrace {M _ {0 , t} ^ {\top} \left[ \frac {\operatorname {d i a g} (R _ {1})}{\operatorname {d i a g} (R _ {2})} \right] _ {\operatorname {d i a g}} M _ {0 , t}} _ {:= \Lambda_ {\mathbf {x} _ {1}}} \mathbf {x} _ {1}. \\ \end{array}
$$

We can evaluate each term separately.

Bounding  $\operatorname{tr}(\Lambda_{\overline{\mathbf{g}}})$ . Let us recall

$$
M _ {K, t} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \mathsf {T o e p} _ {0, t, t - 1} (A _ {K}) & \sigma_ {u} \mathsf {T o e p} _ {- 1, t, t} (A _ {K}) \mathrm {d i a g} _ {t} (B _ {\star}) \\ K \mathsf {T o e p} _ {0, t, t - 1} (A _ {K}) & \sigma_ {u} \mathrm {d i a g} _ {t} (I) + \sigma_ {u} K \mathsf {T o e p} _ {- 1, t, t} (A _ {K}) \mathrm {d i a g} _ {t} (B _ {\star}) \end{array} \right].
$$

Recall  $R_{K} \coloneqq R_{1} + K^{\top}R_{2}K$ . We find that the diagonal terms of  $\Lambda_{\overline{\mathbf{g}}}$  coincide with the diagonals of the matrix  $\Lambda_{\overline{\mathbf{g}},\mathrm{diag}}$  defined as

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathsf {T o e p} _ {0, t, t - 1} (A _ {K}) ^ {\top} \mathrm {d i a g} _ {t - 1} (R _ {K}) \mathsf {T o e p} _ {0, t, t - 1} (A _ {K}) \\ \hline \sigma_ {u} ^ {2} \mathrm {d i a g} _ {t} (B _ {\star}) ^ {\top} \mathsf {T o e p} _ {- 1, t, t} (A _ {K}) ^ {\top} \mathrm {d i a g} _ {t} (R _ {K}) \mathsf {T o e p} _ {- 1, t, t} (A _ {K}) \mathrm {d i a g} _ {t} (B _ {\star}) + \sigma_ {u} ^ {2} \mathrm {d i a g} _ {t} (R _ {2}) + (\text {c r o s s t e r m}) \end{array} \right] _ {\text {d i a g}} \\ \preceq \left[ \frac {\mathsf {T o e p} _ {0 , t , t - 1} (A _ {K}) ^ {\top} \mathrm {d i a g} _ {t - 1} (R _ {K}) \mathsf {T o e p} _ {0 , t , t - 1} (A _ {K})}{2 \sigma_ {u} ^ {2} \mathrm {d i a g} _ {t} (B _ {\star}) ^ {\top} \mathsf {T o e p} _ {- 1 , t , t} (A _ {K}) ^ {\top} \mathrm {d i a g} _ {t} (R _ {K}) \mathsf {T o e p} _ {- 1 , t , t} (A _ {K}) \mathrm {d i a g} _ {t} (B _ {\star}) + 2 \sigma_ {u} ^ {2} \mathrm {d i a g} _ {t} (R _ {2})} \right] _ {\mathrm {d i a g}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where (cross term) denotes the cross term between the term  $\sigma_u^2\mathrm{diag}_t(B_\star)^\top \mathsf{T}\mathsf{o}\mathsf{e}\mathsf{p}_{-1,t,t}(A_K)^\top \mathrm{diag}_t(R_K)\mathsf{T}\mathsf{o}\mathsf{e}\mathsf{p}_{-t,t,t}(A_K)\mathrm{d}$ $\sigma_u^2\mathrm{diag}_t(R_2)$ , which we bound in the second inequality by Young's inequality.

By Lemma G.13, we have

$$
\mathrm {t r} (\mathsf {T o e p} _ {0, t, t - 1} (A _ {K}) ^ {\top} \mathrm {d i a g} _ {t - 1} (R _ {K}) \mathsf {T o e p} _ {0, t, t - 1} (A _ {K})) \leq t \cdot \mathrm {t r} (\mathsf {d l y a p} (A _ {K}, R _ {K})) = J _ {K}.
$$

Similarly, since  $\mathsf{dlyap}(A_K,R_K) = P_K$ , and thus  $\mathrm{rank}(P_K)\leq \mathrm{rank}(R_K)\leq \mathrm{rank}(R_1) + \mathrm{rank}(R_2)$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathrm {t r} (\mathrm {d i a g} _ {t} (B _ {\star}) ^ {\top} \mathsf {T o e p} _ {- 1, t, t} (A _ {K})) ^ {\top} \mathrm {d i a g} _ {t} (R _ {K}) \mathsf {T o e p} _ {- 1, t, t} (A _ {K})) \mathrm {d i a g} _ {t} (B _ {\star})) \\ \leq t \cdot \operatorname {t r} \left(B _ {\star} ^ {\top} \mathrm {d l y a p} \left(A _ {K}, R _ {K}\right) B _ {\star}\right) \\ = t \cdot \mathrm {t r} (B _ {\star} ^ {\top} P _ {K} B _ {\star}) \\ \leq t \| B _ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2} \| P _ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \min \{\mathrm {r a n k} (B _ {\star}), \mathrm {r a n k} (P _ {K}) \} \leq t d _ {\mathrm {e f f}} \| B _ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2} \| P _ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Finally, we can bound  $\mathrm{tr}(2\sigma_u^2\mathrm{diag}_t(R_2))\leq 2t\sigma_u^2\mathrm{rank}(R_2)\| R_2\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 2d_{\mathrm{eff}}t\sigma_u^2\| R_2\|_{\mathrm{op}}$  yielding

$$
\mathrm {t r} (\Lambda_ {\overline {{\mathbf {g}}}}) = \mathrm {t r} (\Lambda_ {\overline {{\mathbf {g}}}, \mathrm {d i a g}}) \leq t J _ {k} + 2 \sigma_ {u} ^ {2} t d _ {\mathrm {e f f}} \left(\| R _ {2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} + \| B _ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2} \| P _ {K} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}\right).
$$

Bounding  $\| \Lambda_{\overline{\mathbf{g}}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}$  .Observe that, for any PSD matrix  $M = \left[ \begin{array}{ll}A & X\\ X^{\top} & B \end{array} \right],$  we have that

$$
M \preceq 2 \left[ \frac {A}{B} \right] _ {\mathrm {d i a g}}.
$$

Since  $\Lambda_{\overline{\mathbf{g}}}\succeq 0$  (it is a non-negative form), in particular, we have  $\Lambda_{\overline{\mathbf{g}}}\preceq 2\Lambda_{\overline{\mathbf{g}},\mathrm{diag}}$ . Thus

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \Lambda_ {\overline {{\mathbf {g}}}} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \lesssim \| \Lambda_ {\overline {{\mathbf {g}}}, \mathrm {d i a g}} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \\ \lesssim \sigma_ {u} ^ {2} \left(\| R _ {2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2} + \| R _ {K} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \| B _ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2} \| \| \mathsf {T o e p} _ {- 1, t, t} (A _ {K}) \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2}\right) + \| R _ {K} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \| \mathsf {T o e p} _ {0, t, t - 1} (A _ {K}) \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Since we can bound  $\| \mathsf{Toep}_{-1,t,t}(A_K)\|_{\mathrm{op}}^2\leq \| A_K\|_{\mathcal{H}_\infty}$  by Lemma G.13, we obtain

$$
\| \Lambda_ {\overline {{\mathbf {g}}}} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \lesssim \| R _ {K} \| \| A _ {K} \| _ {\mathcal {H} _ {\infty}} ^ {2} + \sigma_ {u} ^ {2} \left(\| R _ {2} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} + \| R _ {K} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \| B _ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \| A _ {K} \| _ {\mathcal {H} _ {\infty}} ^ {2}\right),
$$

where we use that  $\sigma_u\leq 1$

Bounding  $\Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_1}$ . Let us recall that

$$
M _ {0, t} := \left[ \begin{array}{c} I _ {t} \\ \mathrm {d i a g} _ {t} (K) \end{array} \right] \mathsf {T o e p C o l} _ {1, t} (A _ {K}).
$$

Thus,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \Lambda_ {\mathbf {x} _ {1}} = M _ {0, t} ^ {\top} \left[ \frac {\mathrm {d i a g} (R _ {1})}{\mathrm {d i a g} (R _ {2})} \right] _ {\mathrm {d i a g}} M _ {0, t} = \mathsf {T o e p C o l} _ {1, t} (A _ {K}) ^ {\top} \mathrm {d i a g} _ {t} (R _ {1} + K ^ {\top} R _ {2} K) \mathsf {T o e p C o l} _ {1, t} (A _ {K}) \\ = \operatorname {T o e p C o l} _ {1, t} \left(A _ {K}\right) ^ {\top} \operatorname {d i a g} _ {t} \left(R _ {K}\right) \operatorname {T o e p C o l} _ {1, t} \left(A _ {K}\right) \\ \preceq \mathrm {d} | \mathrm {y a p} (A _ {K}, R _ {K}) = P _ {K}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Bounding  $\Lambda_{\mathrm{cross}}$ . We can directly verify that there exists a matrix  $A$  with  $AA^{\top} = \Lambda_{\overline{\mathbf{g}}}$  and a matrix  $B$  with  $BB^{\top} = \Lambda_{\mathbf{x}_1}$  such that  $\Lambda_{\mathrm{cross}} = 2AB^{\top}$ . Hence,

$$
\| \Lambda_ {\mathrm {c r o s s}} x _ {1} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \sqrt {\| \Lambda_ {\overline {{\mathbf {g}}}} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \cdot x _ {1} ^ {\top} \Lambda_ {\mathbf {x} _ {1}} x _ {1}} \leq \sqrt {\| \Lambda_ {\overline {{\mathbf {g}}}} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \cdot x _ {1} ^ {\top} P _ {K} x _ {1}}.
$$

# G.7 Proof of Lemma G.2

Set  $\overline{\mathbf{g}} = \begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{w}_{[t - 1]}\\ \mathbf{g}_{[t - 1]} \end{bmatrix}$ . Then we have

$$
\mathbf {x} _ {t} - A _ {K} ^ {t - 1} \mathbf {x} _ {1} = \mathsf {T o e p C o l} _ {1, t - 1} (A _ {K}) \mathbf {w} _ {[ t - 1 ]} + \sigma_ {u} \mathsf {T o e p C o l} _ {1, t - 1} (A _ {K}) \mathrm {d i a g} (B _ {\star}) \mathbf {g} _ {[ t ]} \mathbf {g} _ {[ t ]}.
$$

We now observe that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} \left[ \left\| \mathbf {x} _ {t} - A _ {K} ^ {t - 1} \mathbf {x} _ {1} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \mid \mathbf {x} _ {1} \right] = \operatorname {t r} \left(\operatorname {T o e p C o l} _ {1, t - 1} \left(A _ {K}\right) \operatorname {T o e p C o l} _ {1, t - 1} \left(A _ {K}\right) ^ {\top}\right) \\ + \sigma_ {u} ^ {2} \mathrm {t r} (\mathrm {d i a g} _ {t - 1} (B _ {\star} ^ {\top}) \mathrm {T o e p C o l} _ {1, t - 1} (A _ {K}) \mathrm {T o e p C o l} _ {1, t - 1} (A _ {K}) ^ {\top} \mathrm {d i a g} _ {t - 1} (B _ {\star})) \\ \leq \left(1 + \sigma_ {u} ^ {2} \| B _ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2}\right) \operatorname {t r} \left(\operatorname {T o e p C o l} _ {1, t - 1} \left(A _ {K}\right) \operatorname {T o e p C o l} _ {1, t - 1} \left(A _ {K}\right) ^ {\top}\right) \\ \leq \left(1 + \sigma_ {u} ^ {2} \| B _ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2}\right) \| \text {T o e p C o l} _ {1, t - 1} \left(A _ {K}\right) \| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \\ \leq \left(1 + \sigma_ {u} ^ {2} \| B _ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2}\right) \operatorname {t r} \left(\mathrm {d l y a p} \left(A _ {K}, I\right)\right) \\ \leq \left(1 + \sigma_ {u} ^ {2} \| B _ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2}\right) J _ {K}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last inequality uses Lemma B.5. Since  $\mathbf{x}_t - A_K^{t-1}\mathbf{x}_1$  is a Gaussian quadratic form, the simplified Hanson Wright inequality (Corollary 6) gives

$$
\| \mathbf {x} _ {t} - A _ {K} ^ {t - 1} \mathbf {x} _ {1} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \lesssim (1 + \sigma_ {u} ^ {2} \| B _ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2}) J _ {K} \log \frac {1}{\delta}.
$$

# G.8 Extension to General Noise Models

Our upper bounds hold for general noise distributions with the following properties:

1. The noise satisfies a Hanson-Wright style inequality, so that an analogue of Lemma G.1 holds. Recall that Lemma G.1 establishes that the true costs concentrate around their expectations.  
2. The noise process is a  $\sigma_{+}$ -sub-Gaussian martingale difference sequence, in the sense that  $\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{w}_t\mid \mathbf{w}_1,\ldots ,\mathbf{w}_{t - 1}] = 0$  and for any  $v\in \mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathbf{x}}}$ ,  $\mathbb{E}[\exp (\langle v,\mathbf{w}_t\rangle \mid \mathbf{w}_1,\ldots ,\mathbf{w}_{t - 1}]\leq \exp (\frac{1}{2}\| v\| ^2\sigma_+^2)$ . This is necessary for the self-normalized tail bound (Lemma E.1 of Abbasi-Yadkori et al. [2011]).  
3. The noise satisfies the block-martingale small ball condition from Simchowitz et al. [2018], which ensures the covariates are well-conditioned during the estimation phase (in particular, that an analogue of Lemma E.4 holds)

In more detail, suppose that the noise is  $\sigma_{+}$ -sub-Gaussian, and that  $\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{w}_t\mathbf{w}_t^\top \mid \mathbf{w}_1,\dots ,\mathbf{w}_{t - 1}]\succeq \Sigma_{-}\succ 0$ . Then by applying the Paley-Zygmund inequality (analogously to Eq. 3.12 in Simchowitz et al. [2018]), one can show that the  $(1,\frac{1}{2}\Sigma_{-},p)$ -block-martingale small-ball property holds with

$$
\begin{array}{l} p = \frac {1}{4} \cdot \min  _ {v \neq 0} \frac {\mathbb {E} \left[ \langle \mathbf {w} _ {t} , z \rangle^ {2} \mid \mathbf {w} _ {1 : t - 1} \right] ^ {2}}{\mathbb {E} \left[ \langle \mathbf {w} _ {t} , z \rangle^ {4} \mid \mathbf {w} _ {1 : t - 1} \right]} \\ \gtrsim \frac {\lambda_ {\mathrm {m i n}} (\Sigma_ {-}) ^ {2}}{\sigma_ {+} ^ {4}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where in the last inequality, we upper bound  $\mathbb{E}[\langle \mathbf{w}_t,z\rangle^4 ]$  using the standard moment bound for sub-Gaussian variables. Hence, a sub-Gaussian upper bound and covariance lower bound are enough to guarantee point 3 above holds.

Point 1 is more delicate, because Hanson-Wright inequalities are known under only restrictive assumptions: namely, for vectors which have independent sub-Gaussian coordinates [Rudelson et al., 2013], or for those satisfying a Lipschitz-concentration property [Adamczak et al., 2015]. For the first condition to be satisfied, we need to assume that there exists a matrix  $\Sigma_{+} \succ 0$  such that the vectors  $\tilde{\mathbf{w}}_t \coloneqq \Sigma_{+}^{-1/2}\mathbf{w}_t$  are (a) jointly independent, and (b) have jointly independent, sub-Gaussian coordinates. For the second condition to hold, we must assume that the concatenated vectors  $(\tilde{\mathbf{w}}_1, \ldots, \tilde{\mathbf{w}}_t)$  satisfy the Lipschitz-concentration property [Adamczak et al., 2015, Definition 2.1]. If either condition holds, then we can obtain the same regret as in our main theorem by modifying Lemma G.12 to use a quadratic form for the sequence  $(\tilde{\mathbf{w}}_1, \ldots, \tilde{\mathbf{w}}_t)$ , and then applying one of the Hanson-Wright variants above to attain Lemma G.1.

In general, it is not known if sub-Gaussian martingale noise satisfies a Hanson-Wright inequality. In this case, we can demonstrate the concentration of costs around their expectation via a combination of the Azuma-Hoeffding/Azuma-Bernstein inequality with truncation and mixing arguments. This type of argument bounds the fluctuations of the costs around their mean as roughly  $(d_{\mathbf{x}} + d_{\mathbf{u}})\sqrt{T}$ ,

which is worse than the square root scaling  $\sqrt{d_{\mathbf{x}} + d_{\mathbf{u}}}\cdot \sqrt{T}$  enjoyed by the Hanson-Wright inequality. Up to logarithmic factors, this would yield regret of  $(d_{\mathbf{x}} + d_{\mathbf{u}})\sqrt{T} +\sqrt{d_{\mathbf{x}}d_{\mathbf{u}}^2T} = \sqrt{d_{\mathbf{x}}\max\{d_{\mathbf{x}},d_{\mathbf{u}}^2\}T}$ , which is sub-optimal for  $d_{\mathbf{x}}\gg d_{\mathbf{u}}^{2}$ . It is not clear if any algorithm can do better in this regime (without a sharper inequality for the concentration of costs around their means), since it is not clear how to ameliorate these random fluctuations. Nevertheless, the final regret bound of  $\sqrt{d_{\mathbf{x}}\max\{d_{\mathbf{x}},d_{\mathbf{u}}^2\}T}$  still improves upon the dimension dependence in the upper bound of  $\sqrt{(d_{\mathbf{x}} + d_{\mathbf{u}})^3T}$  attained by Mania et al. [2019].
